These documents had a visible relationship: they shared real, readable, common code with the indexed text. The filter was working exactly as specified. The specification was the problem.

Cause one: the normaliser is built for prose

The previous chapter described the byte translation table every document and every benchmark row passes through before any hashing happens: A-Z lowercased, a-z and 0-9 kept, every other byte replaced by a space.

_XLAT = np.full(256, 32, dtype=np.uint8)          # everything -> space
for _c in range(ord("a"), ord("z") + 1):
    _XLAT[_c] = _c
for _c in range(ord("A"), ord("Z") + 1):
    _XLAT[_c] = _c + 32                            # A-Z lowercased
for _c in range(ord("0"), ord("9") + 1):
    _XLAT[_c] = _c

For prose that is the right choice, and it is what makes the filter match copies rather than only originals. A benchmark question that has passed through a content management system, a PDF extractor and a blog theme differs from the original in case, punctuation and whitespace, and stripping those three away is precisely what lets the match survive.

For code it removes most of the signal. Punctuation in Python is not decoration. Commas, colons, parentheses and brackets carry the structure, and the identifiers that remain are drawn from a small shared vocabulary of for, in, range, def, self, return, len, i, x, n. Consider one line:

for i, a in enumerate(numbers):      ->    for i a in enumerate numbers

Six words survive from a line that was structurally specific. Chain a few such lines together and the 13-word window that results is not a fingerprint of any particular file. It is a fingerprint of Python.

Cause two: we indexed the test harness

The second cause is a choice in the benchmark registry rather than in the hashing.

Deciding which columns of a benchmark row go into the index is a judgement about what a contaminated document would actually contain. For HellaSwag and PIQA, the answer options are the benchmark, so they must be indexed. For GSM8K, the worked solution is the leak. For TriviaQA, the answer aliases are single entities like a city name, so only the questions are indexed. For HumanEval, the original registry entry indexed the prompt, the canonical solution, and the test column.

The test column is not benchmark content in the sense the other columns are. It is the harness that checks a candidate solution, and across all 164 problems it is close to the same few lines of scaffolding: a check function, a candidate argument, a sequence of asserts. Indexing it puts that scaffolding into the index 164 times over, and then normalisation strips out the punctuation that distinguished one instance of it from another.

The result is a set of index entries that are, after normalisation, generic assertion boilerplate. Any Python file in the corpus containing a test suite of a similar shape matches them, and matches them repeatedly, because the pattern recurs throughout a single file.

Two causes that multiply

Either cause alone would have raised HumanEval's rate. Together they multiply, and the multiplication is what produces a figure like 88.78 rather than something in the range of two or three.

Normalisation lowers the specificity of every code n-gram in the index. The test column then fills the index with the least specific code in the benchmark, repeated. The rate is a count of matching n-grams rather than matching documents, so a single ordinary Python file with a handful of test functions contributes many hits, and the denominator is small because HumanEval is only 164 problems.2 The worker accumulates hits_by_benchmark by adding the number of matching windows, not by adding one per dropped document. That is the correct choice for a diagnostic, since it distinguishes a document that shares one window from one that shares fifty, and it is the reason a rate above 1.0 is arithmetically possible at all. Every term in that arithmetic pushes in the same direction.

The registry entry now records the reasoning next to the fields, so that a future reader does not have to reconstruct it:

Benchmark(
    "humaneval",
    [Candidate("openai/openai_humaneval", None, "test"),
     Candidate("openai_humaneval", None, "test")],
    # `test` DROPPED after measurement. It is near-identical scaffolding
    # across all 164 problems (`def check(candidate): assert candidate(...)`),
    # so once normalisation strips punctuation its 13-grams match ordinary
    # Python everywhere.
    fields=["prompt", "canonical_solution"],
    note="test scaffolding excluded — it generated boilerplate false positives",
)

What it cost, and what we did

The question that decides the response is not how badly the filter misbehaved. It is how much corpus was lost.

The Python source removed 28,968 documents from 12,866,649 scanned, a rate of 0.2251%, and wrote 14.90B tokens. If every one of those removals were a false positive attributable to HumanEval, the damage is a fifth of one percent of the code corpus. There is no plausible mechanism by which losing 0.225% of one source, selected by a criterion uncorrelated with quality, changes what the model learns. Re-ingesting the source would have cost real compute and real time to recover something that is not measurable at the far end.3 The cost of being wrong in the other direction was also bounded here. HumanEval and GSM8K are post-training benchmarks and were deliberately not run on r1, which is a base model that has only ever done next-token prediction. Removing too much Python on HumanEval's account could not have flattered a HumanEval score, because no HumanEval score was ever produced.

So no re-ingest was performed. Two changes went into the code for any future run. The first is the field list above, with test dropped. The second is a threshold on NgramIndex.check, so that a corpus can require several matching n-grams before a document is discarded rather than one:

def check(self, text: str, n: int = NGRAM_N,
          min_matches: int = 1) -> tuple[bool, dict[str, int]]:
    """(contaminated, {benchmark: n_matching_ngrams}).

    `min_matches` guards against incidental collisions. One shared 13-gram is
    decisive for prose but not for code: normalisation strips punctuation, so
    de-punctuated Python idiom collides across unrelated files. Raising this
    for code corpora trades a little recall for a lot of precision.
    """

The default stays at 1, so prose sources behave as before and the change is opt-in per corpus. Neither fix has been re-measured against the corpus. The 104.80B-token corpus, from which r1 drew the 5,000,003,584 tokens it trained on, was produced by the pipeline as it stood during the run, with the test column indexed and a single matching n-gram sufficient to drop a document. What is in this chapter is a diagnosis and a code change, not a second measurement, and the two should not be read as the same thing.

The limit that has nothing to do with code

The same report carries a second scope limit, and it belongs next to every benchmark number in this book rather than in a footnote at the back.

A 13-gram filter can only remove text it can index. A benchmark row shorter than 13 words produces no 13-gram at all, so it never enters the index, and no document in the corpus can ever be matched against it. The row is not weakly protected. It is not protected.

The extractor counts those rows separately for exactly this reason, and the report emits the count per benchmark:

coverage[name] = {
    "rows": rows,
    "rows_indexed": rows - short,
    "rows_too_short": short,
    "coverage": (rows - short) / rows if rows else 0.0,
    "ngrams": s.get("ngrams", 0),
}

TriviaQA is 49% covered. Of its 17,944 rows, 9,163 are too short to yield a single 13-gram, which follows directly from the shape of the set: a TriviaQA question is usually one line, and only the questions are indexed because the answer aliases are single entities. PIQA is the next lowest at 92%. Five of the eight sets sit at 99 to 100%.

This is not a defect that can be repaired by tuning. It is what a 13-gram method is. A shorter n would cover more rows and would also match ordinary English, which trades a stated limit for an unstated flood of false positives of exactly the kind this chapter has just finished diagnosing. The workable response is to publish the coverage number beside the accuracy number, so a reader can see which claim is fully supported and which is half supported.

WinoGrande's zero

One set recorded no matches at all, and the report warned about it, because a benchmark with zero hits across 107 million documents usually means a broken extractor rather than a clean corpus. The gate treats that as a warning rather than a failure:

if decon["benchmarks_with_zero_hits"]:
    warns.append(f"no n-gram hits for {decon['benchmarks_with_zero_hits']} — "
                 "check those extractors before trusting their scores")

The warning fired on WinoGrande, and the zero is plausibly genuine. WinoGrande sentences are short, and the two options differ by a single word, so the indexed text is both small and highly specific. The index self-check gives some assurance that extraction worked, since every benchmark row tested against the index it was built from was detected, 1,414 of 1,414.4 The self-check exists because a renamed column is the quiet way decontamination becomes a no-op. The extractor still runs, still produces rows, and silently indexes empty strings. Building the index and then asking it to find its own source rows catches that in seconds. What the check cannot tell us is whether a longer WinoGrande row would have matched something, because there were few long rows to begin with.

We report it as a warning that was raised, examined, and left standing rather than as a result.

What this generalises to

Three things from this episode transfer to any filter, in any pipeline.

A component that fires far more than its peers is measuring something other than what you named it after. The name on the filter was "HumanEval contamination". What it measured was "resembles de-punctuated Python". Those two quantities agree on the benchmark rows and diverge everywhere else, and the divergence only becomes visible when the rate is compared against sibling rates in the same run. Comparing HumanEval's match count to its own expectation would have taught us nothing, because nobody had an expectation for it. Comparing it to seven other sets measured identically took one column of arithmetic.

Look at what was removed before deciding what the number means. The sample of dropped documents cost nothing to keep and settled a question that no amount of further reasoning about hash functions would have settled. Any filter that discards data should retain a readable sample of what it discarded, with attribution, because the sample is the only evidence that distinguishes a working filter from a filter that works on the wrong thing.

A high catch rate is not a result. It is easy to read 88.78 as the filter succeeding, and to write the sentence about how much contamination was caught in the Python corpus. That sentence would have been in this book, it would have been wrong, and nothing downstream would ever have contradicted it, because the benchmark it referred to was never run. The measurement that mattered was not the one that looked impressive. It was the ratio of removed documents to scanned documents in the affected source, which came to 0.225%, and told us the correct response was to fix the code, write down that the fix is unverified, and move on.

Section 02 · The Data

12. The tokenizer we did not train

⏱ 10 min 📊 5 figures 163,840

Keeping K3's own tiktoken BPE, the byte-for-byte round-trip gate, the literal [EOS] trap that would have injected document breaks mid-page, and why shards are uint32.

Most pretraining projects at this scale train their own tokenizer. It is cheap, it takes an afternoon, and a vocabulary fitted to the corpus gives a smaller embedding table and better compression on the text that matters. For a 1.02-billion-parameter model, a much smaller vocabulary would have been the obvious choice.

We used K3's, unchanged, all 163,840 entries of it. This chapter is about the door that decision keeps open, what it cost in parameters, and the two checks that stand between a tokenizer you believe and a corpus that is quietly wrong.

The reason is a stage that has not happened yet

Reasoning distillation from a large teacher to a small student operates on token distributions. The teacher produces a probability over its vocabulary at each position, the student is trained to match it, and the two vocabularies have to be the same object for that loss to be defined. With a smaller vocabulary of our own there would be no correspondence between the teacher's index 47,203 and anything in the student's table, and the remaining option would be distilling on text rather than on distributions, which discards most of the teacher's signal.

No distillation was performed in this project. r1 is a base model that has only ever done next-token prediction, and we say so wherever the subject comes up. The decision was made anyway, because a vocabulary cannot be revisited later. Changing it invalidates every checkpoint and the whole 104.80-billion-token tokenized corpus.

The bill, in full

A tied embedding costs vocab x hidden parameters and runs on every token in both directions, as the input lookup and as the output projection. At r1's hidden size of 512, that is 163,840 x 512 = 83.9M parameters against a total active count of 145M. The vocabulary is 58% of everything r1 computes per token.

The chapter on scaling down works through what that does to the ladder's design. The comparison worth repeating here is that the same parameter counter, run on K3's released configuration, puts embeddings at 1.1% of K3's active parameters. The same tokenizer, on the model it was built for, is a rounding error; on ours it is the majority of the model.

That is not an argument against the decision; it is the price of it, and it is why the rungs of this ladder are defined on non-embedding active parameters (61M for r1) rather than on the headline figure.

Rebuilding the encoding rather than calling the wrapper

Stage 1 tokenizes on the order of a hundred billion tokens. Moonshot ships a PreTrainedTokenizer subclass, tokenization_kimi.TikTokenTokenizer, which pays Python overhead per document, while the tiktoken.Encoding underneath it releases the GIL and batches across Rust threads. So data/k3_tokenizer.py rebuilds the same Encoding from the same files:

mergeable_ranks = load_tiktoken_bpe(str(d / "tiktoken.model"))
num_base = len(mergeable_ranks)

cfg = json.loads((d / "tokenizer_config.json").read_text())
named = {int(k): v["content"] for k, v in cfg.get("added_tokens_decoder", {}).items()}

special_tokens = {
    named.get(i, f"<|reserved_token_{i}|>"): i
    for i in range(num_base, num_base + NUM_RESERVED_SPECIAL_TOKENS)
}

enc = tiktoken.Encoding(name="tiktoken.model", pat_str=PAT_STR,
                        mergeable_ranks=mergeable_ranks,
                        special_tokens=special_tokens)
if enc.n_vocab != VOCAB_SIZE:
    raise ValueError(f"expected vocab {VOCAB_SIZE}, got {enc.n_vocab}")

The vocabulary is 163,584 base merges plus 256 reserved special ids, which is where 163,840 comes from. [BOS] is 163584 and [EOS] is 163585, the first two of the reserved block.

Rebuilding rather than wrapping means the pre-tokenization regex has to be copied verbatim, including parts of it that do not do what they appear to do.1 PAT_STR contains character-class intersections written in Java syntax, a class of letter and mark categories intersected with && against a negated \p{Han} class. tiktoken's regex engine does not implement && as intersection, so the class does not mean what its author intended. It must still be passed through unchanged, because the released tokenizer was trained and shipped against this literal pattern. A "corrected" pattern would produce a different segmentation and a different model.

Anything rebuilt from parts has to be checked against the thing it replaces, which is what the rest of this chapter is.

The gate

PLAN.md sets one requirement for the tokenizer: the decode round-trip matches. data/verify_tokenizer.py implements that as a short list of checks, ordered by how badly each failure would damage the run.

The first is round-trip equality, decode(encode(x)) == x, byte for byte, across 10 samples covering the ways text differs: English prose, Chinese, code, LaTeX, whitespace, marker text, and one unbroken run of 60,000 non-whitespace characters.

That last sample is a stress probe rather than a piece of text. tiktoken's Rust extension raises a PanicException on very long inputs and on long runs of consecutive non-whitespace characters, and a web crawl and a code corpus both contain exactly that shape: a minified bundle, a base64 blob, a long hash. The shipped wrapper guards against it by splitting a document into encode-safe pieces before handing it to the Rust extension, and k3_tokenizer.py ports the same guard, with the short-document case returning the text unsplit so that the common path allocates nothing.

Without the guard, one pathological document takes down an ingest worker, and a worker that dies halfway leaves a partial shard behind. The check sits in the gate rather than in a comment because the failure appears at ingest time, on real crawled data, where finding out costs hours of CPU.

The remaining checks are quieter. Every id must be below 163,840, so that a uint32 shard is lossless and the embedding table is the size we budgeted. [EOS] must appear exactly once per document and never inside a document body. Bytes per token, aggregated across the content samples, must land inside a plausible range for well-trained BPE; the 60,000-character run is excluded, because letting a synthetic probe move a content statistic would make the check meaningless.2 The exclusion list is one entry long and lives in the source as FERTILITY_EXCLUDE = {"long_run"}. It is a small thing that generalises: a test corpus usually contains both representative samples and adversarial ones, and any statistic computed over the union of the two describes neither.

The last check is agreement with the shipped wrapper, and it is the one worth the rest of the chapter.

The literal [EOS] trap

Our rebuilt encoder must produce the same ids as Moonshot's wrapper, or we are training on a different tokenization than the model was designed for. The check is a straight comparison across the samples, and it passes: all 10 identical, called as hf.encode(t, allow_special_tokens=False). The argument in that call is the interesting part.

TikTokenTokenizer.encode defaults to allow_special_tokens=True. Under that default, a document containing the five characters [EOS] does not tokenize to those characters. It tokenizes to token 163585, the control token our shard format uses to mark where one document ends and the next begins.

For a chat model that is correct: markers in a chat template are supposed to become control tokens. For a pretraining crawl it is a corruption. One web page discussing tokenizer markers is enough for the ingest to write a document separator into the middle of a training sequence. The model then learns that documents sometimes end mid-sentence and resume on the same topic, and every downstream number stays plausible.

encode_ordinary_batch never emits a special token at all, so our encoder cannot produce this failure. We append [EOS] ourselves, once, after each document.

What the gate should then assert took some thought. A test that only checked agreement would fail as soon as we adopted the behaviour a crawl needs, because the two encoders are supposed to differ under the wrapper's default. A test that only checked our own behaviour would not notice if we had drifted away from K3's tokenization. So verify_tokenizer.py asserts both:

# like-for-like: the wrapper called the way we call ours
mismatched = []
for n, t, ids in zip(names, texts, encoded):
    if hf.encode(t, allow_special_tokens=False) != ids[:-1]:
        mismatched.append(n)

# and the divergence we chose, asserted rather than assumed
wrapper_default = hf.encode("[EOS]")
ours = encode_documents(["[EOS]"], num_threads=1)[0][:-1]
if sp["eos"] not in wrapper_default:
    fails.append("wrapper default no longer promotes literal '[EOS]' — "
                 "re-check whether our divergence is still needed")
if sp["eos"] in ours:
    fails.append("our encoder promoted literal '[EOS]' to a control token")

The second half of that is a test that fails when the upstream behaviour changes. If a future release of the wrapper stops promoting marker text, our divergence is no longer needed and the extra code is a liability, and the gate says so rather than continuing to pass silently.3 There is a further trap in the same method, recorded in a comment beside the call. TikTokenTokenizer.encode routes to super().encode the moment any additional keyword argument is passed, and the parent implementation has never heard of allow_special_tokens. The argument is then silently ignored and literal marker text becomes a control token again. The like-for-like comparison therefore passes allow_special_tokens=False and no other kwargs at all, which is a constraint on the call site that no signature communicates.

The shard format, and one byte width that matters

Tokenized output lands at /data/tokens/<source>/<source>-w<worker>-s<seq>.bin as raw little-endian uint32 with no header. Document boundaries are marked by EOS, token 163585, and a .json sidecar carries the token count.

Each of those choices exists for a reason, and the reasons are about what the dataloader has to do when it resumes from a checkpoint deep into a run.

No header means an offset into the file is an offset into the token stream, with no arithmetic. The dataloader's cursor is part of the checkpoint, and restoring it is a seek.

A sidecar rather than a header means the token count can be read without opening the data file, which is what makes the mix planner cheap: mixes.py sizes each source from measured tokens per file across the 1,063 shards by reading only the small JSON files.

EOS as the boundary carries document structure in-band, in the same array as the text, so a shard is a single flat object with no second file that can go out of sync with it.

uint32 rather than uint16 is the choice with teeth. uint16 holds values up to 65,535 and our vocabulary is 163,840, so every id above 65,535 would wrap silently on write, producing a file that opens correctly, has the right length, contains only valid ids, decodes to text and trains without error. No exception, no assertion, no shape mismatch anywhere downstream. The corruption is in the data, and it looks exactly like data.

The cost of that width is recurring. Four bytes per token across a 104.80-billion-token corpus makes storage, not compute, the line item that keeps arriving at $0.09 per GiB per month. The full ingest pass cost about $17 of CPU; the storage has been billed ever since.

The histogram, with thresholds fixed first

The last part of the Stage 1 gate is a token histogram over a sample of the finished shards, reporting three numbers:

statisticmeasuredgated in report.SANITY
vocabulary coverage61.7%a floor
unigram entropy11.74 bitsreported, not gated
most frequent token's share3.54%a ceiling

The most frequent token is a comma.

The floor and the ceiling were written into report.SANITY before any of the numbers existed, which is the only arrangement under which a threshold means anything. A coverage floor chosen after seeing 61.7% describes the result rather than testing it.

Each number checks a specific failure. Coverage asks whether the corpus is as broad as the tokenizer assumes. A frontier multilingual vocabulary applied to a mostly-English corpus will not use all of itself, and 61.7% describes that mismatch fairly. Had it come back very low, the embedding tax would be buying almost nothing.

Top-token share is the check for a stuck separator. If the ingest were writing EOS after every line rather than every document, or if a boilerplate page were repeated across a source, one id would take an implausible fraction of the corpus. At 3.54% for a comma, the distribution has the shape English text has.

Unigram entropy at 11.74 bits is a summary of the distribution rather than a pass/fail gate, and it is worth reading against the loss curve. A uniform distribution over 163,840 tokens carries log2(163,840) = 17.32 bits. The corpus, measured on unigram frequencies with no context at all, carries 11.74. Our run finished at a cross-entropy of 2.623 nats, about 3.78 bits per token. The gap between 11.74 and 3.78 is what 5.00 billion tokens of training bought, and it shows how much of a language model's work is done by context rather than by frequency.

One limitation belongs in the prose rather than only in the code. The histogram is computed over a sample of shards, not the whole corpus, so 61.7% is a sampled figure. Coverage across all 104.80 billion tokens would be a defensible thing to measure and we did not measure it.4 The sampler takes shards at a stride across the sorted shard list and reads a bounded number of tokens from each, which spreads the sample across sources rather than concentrating it in whichever source sorts first. Coverage measured this way can only under-report, since a token appearing in an unsampled shard and nowhere else counts as unused. The reported 61.7% is a lower bound, which is the direction a gate should err in.

What this chapter is really about

The tokenizer decision itself took one line in the plan and produced no numbers beyond the 83.9M-parameter bill. The two checks around it are the part worth carrying elsewhere.

The first is that a deliberate divergence from a library's default has to be asserted rather than merely implemented. Our encoder differs from the shipped wrapper in one respect, that difference is correct for our use and wrong for the wrapper's, and both halves of that statement are now tested. If the upstream default changes, the gate fails.

The second is the shape of the uint16 failure. Most bugs in a pretraining pipeline announce themselves as a shape mismatch, a NaN or a crash. The dangerous ones produce output that passes every structural check and is wrong in its values. We met three in this project: this one, the manifest bug that would have trained the stable phase on 100% finemath, and the router balancer computed on per-rank load rather than global expert load. That third one belongs to the flagship on 2x B300 rather than to our run; r1 trained on one H200 and was never sharded, so it could not have hit it. None of the three would have raised an error. Each was prevented by a check written for it in advance, by someone asking what a silent version of the failure would look like.

Section 02 · The Data

13. The mix, and the bug that would have trained on one source

⏱ 11 min 📊 6 figures

Stable and decay mixes built from measured tokens-per-file, and a string-splitting bug that pointed four of six sources at files that did not exist while the loader silently renormalised onto the two that survived.

A corpus is not a training set until something decides how much of it the model sees, and when. Ours is 104.80 billion tokens in 1,063 shards across six sources, and the run consumed 5.00 billion of them. Which 5.00 billion, and in what proportions, is the mix.

There are two mixes rather than one, because the schedule has two phases. WSD holds the learning rate flat through a long stable phase and decays it over the final 15%, and the data switches at the same boundary: general web through the stable phase, then maths, code and synthetic reasoning through the decay. The stable mix in r1's run was 59.5% fineweb-edu, 25.5% web-diverse, 10.0% code-python, 3.0% finemath and 2.0% open-web-math.

Those five numbers are the subject of this chapter twice over: how they were arrived at, and the period during which they were a fiction that every dashboard in the system would have kept reporting while the model trained on one source.

Mixes are specified over roles, not over sources

The natural way to write a mix is a dictionary from source name to weight. We did not do that, because of what happens when a source disappears. Two of the six repositories we wanted were gated, and one of them never cleared. Specified directly over sources, losing one means either hand-editing every weight or letting the survivors absorb the gap in whatever proportions renormalisation produces, which would have quietly changed how much web data the model saw.

So a mix is specified at two levels: weights over roles, and weights over the sources that fill each role.

# Stage 3: ~2% warmup, long stable phase, final ~15% decay.
DECAY_FRACTION = 0.15

STABLE_ROLES = {"web": 0.85, "code": 0.10, "math": 0.05, "reasoning": 0.00}
DECAY_ROLES  = {"web": 0.25, "code": 0.25, "math": 0.30, "reasoning": 0.20}

SOURCE_WEIGHTS = {
    "web":       {"fineweb-edu": 0.70, "web-diverse": 0.30},
    "code":      {"code-python": 1.0},
    "math":      {"finemath": 0.6, "open-web-math": 0.4},
    "reasoning": {"cosmopedia": 1.0},
}

# Above this we are re-showing the same tokens rather than adding information.
MAX_EPOCHS = 4.0

A source going missing now rebalances inside its own role and leaves the role weights untouched. If web-diverse had failed to resolve, fineweb-edu would have absorbed its 30% of the web role and the model would still have seen 85% web during the stable phase. The planner records a warning when this happens rather than doing it silently, a distinction this chapter returns to.

The stable mix quoted above falls straight out of these constants: 0.85 × 0.70 = 0.595 for fineweb-edu, 0.85 × 0.30 = 0.255 for web-diverse, 0.10 for code-python, and the maths role's 0.05 split 0.6 / 0.4 into 0.030 and 0.020. Cosmopedia carries the reasoning role, weighted zero during the stable phase, so it appears in one mix and not the other.

The file budget comes from a measurement, not an estimate

A mix in tokens has to become a mix in files, because files are what the ingest workers download and tokenize. The conversion needs tokens-per-file, and there are two ways to get it.

The convenient way is to assume a tokens-per-document figure, multiply by the rows in a parquet file, and move on. That number is wrong in three directions at once. Tokenizers differ, and a document costs a different number of tokens under K3's 163,840-entry BPE than under any other. Decontamination removes documents before they are counted, at a rate varying from 0.0280% on web-diverse to 0.7239% on finemath. And it is wrong for code-python by an order of magnitude, because the ungated fallback repository keeps every language in one directory and 94% of what was downloaded was discarded to extract Python.

The measured way is a dry run. Before the real ingest, a sampling pass reads two files per source through the full pipeline, with the real tokenizer, the real 13-gram filter and the real extraction, and records what came out the other end.1 The dry run also produces the per-benchmark hit counts that later became the HumanEval investigation. Its job here is narrower: how many tokens does one file of this source actually contribute, after everything that removes tokens has run. The file budget is then arithmetic over those measurements:

def file_budget(p, tokens_per_file, files_available):
    """`tokens_per_file` comes from the dry run — measured,
    post-decontamination, with this tokenizer. Assuming it is the trap."""
    for src, want in p["source_tokens"].items():
        tpf = tokens_per_file.get(src)
        if not tpf:
            notes.append(f"{src}: no measurement, cannot size the file budget")
            continue
        need = int(want / tpf) + 1
        have = files_available.get(src, 0)
        if have and need > have:
            epochs = need / have
            ...

Two properties of that function are deliberate. A source with no measurement produces a note and no allocation, rather than a plausible guess. And when the budget asks for more files than exist, the code computes the implied epoch count and refuses above MAX_EPOCHS = 4.0, because a small model trained repeatedly on the same subset memorises it instead of learning from it.

Reserving the decay shards first

The manifests are built over the shards that exist on the volume, and the decay phase is allocated before the stable phase rather than after, because the anneal is the smaller and more valuable claim. If the decay phase re-showed tokens the model had already seen, the final 15% of training would be a second epoch over familiar data rather than fresh maths and code, and the benchmark movement it is supposed to produce would be attributable to neither. So decay shards are taken from the tail of each source's shard list, the stable phase gets what remains, and disjointness is asserted rather than assumed:

stable_names = {n for s in phases["stable"]["sources"].values() for n in s["shards"]}
decay_names  = {n for s in phases["decay"]["sources"].values()  for n in s["shards"]}
overlap = stable_names & decay_names
if overlap:
    raise AssertionError(
        f"{len(overlap)} shards appear in both phases — the anneal would "
        "re-show tokens from the stable phase")

The manifests are 947 stable shards and 94 decay shards, with the assertion passing.

What the loader does with them

The dataloader reads the two manifests and produces packed sequences at 4,096 tokens. Three of its properties matter to the rest of the run.

Sampling is per sequence, not per shard. For every sequence, the loader draws a source from the mix weights and takes the next 4,097 tokens from that source's cursor. Reading one shard to exhaustion before moving to the next would give the model hours of pure code followed by hours of pure web, which is a curriculum nobody chose. Per-sequence sampling keeps the realised mix close to the target at every point in training rather than only in aggregate.

Packing crosses document boundaries. Documents are separated by the EOS token, 163585, and sequences are cut at 4,096 regardless of where those boundaries fall. Nothing is spent on padding, and EOS teaches the model where a document ends.

State is explicit. Each source stream carries a shard index, an offset within that shard and an epoch counter; the loader carries its RNG state and per-source token totals. All of it goes into the checkpoint, so a resume continues from the exact token it stopped at rather than restarting the epoch or silently re-showing tokens.2 The chapter on checkpointing covers how this was verified: resume_equivalence trains past a checkpoint twice and compares per-step losses. It found an off-by-one in the checkpoint's step label, and after the fix the first resumed step is bitwise identical at 0.00e+00.

The bug

The manifest builder walks the .json sidecars on the volume, one per shard, recording each shard's name, token count and path. An earlier version did not use the sidecar's filename directly. It rebuilt the name:

name = source + "-" + stem.split("-", 1)[1]

The intent was normalisation: take the source, take everything after the first hyphen, reassemble. On a source whose name has no hyphen this is the identity function. finemath-w000-s0000 splits into finemath and w000-s0000, and reassembles unchanged.

On a source whose name contains a hyphen it is not. fineweb-edu-w000-s0000 splits at the first hyphen, into fineweb and edu-w000-s0000, and reassembling with the full source name gives fineweb-edu-edu-w000-s0000, a file that has never existed.

Four of our six source names contain a hyphen. Two do not.

Now consider what a dataloader should do when a manifest names a file that is not there.

The failure that was not a crash

The loader's original behaviour was to skip what it could not find. For each source it built the list of shard paths, filtered to the ones that exist, dropped any source left with nothing, renormalised the weights over whatever remained so the probabilities still summed to one, and started training.

That is a reasonable thing for a loader to do if the goal is to keep going. Applied here, with four of six sources resolving to nonexistent paths, the stable mix's five sources reduce to one: cosmopedia is weighted zero during the stable phase, and finemath is the only other unhyphenated name. Its 3.0% weight, renormalised over a set containing only itself, becomes 100%.

The entire stable phase would have trained on finemath.

This is the class of bug that concerns us most in this project, and it is worth naming precisely why.

A crash is a good outcome. It costs an hour and tells you where to look. A wrong number in a report is a middling outcome, because it is at least in a report, where someone may notice it. This bug produces neither. It produces a run that starts normally, trains for 38,147 steps, checkpoints correctly, passes every gate in the monitoring system, ends with a loss curve of the expected shape, and returns a model that is wrong for a reason appearing in none of the metrics being collected.

It would probably have looked better than the real run, too. Our final held-out perplexity by source is 10.24 on finemath against 22.35 on fineweb-edu and 37.74 on web-diverse. A model trained on one narrow maths corpus reports a lower training loss than one trained across six sources, so the number a human watches most closely would have moved in the direction that reads as success. We did not run that experiment, so this is reasoning from the per-source perplexities rather than a measurement. It is enough to make the point: the loss curve is not a control on the data.

Fail loudly on missing inputs

The fix has three parts, and only the first is about the bug.

The name rebuild was deleted. The sidecar's filename is the shard's name, so the manifest builder now uses sidecar.stem directly, with the reason written into the code so nobody normalises it again.

The loader's default behaviour was then inverted. Missing shards are collected during construction, and if any are missing the constructor raises:

# Silently dropping a source and renormalising the rest changes the data
# distribution without changing anything visible. That is how a
# "59.5% fineweb-edu" stable phase became 100% finemath, undetected, in the
# first build of these manifests. Fail loudly by default.
if missing and not allow_missing:
    detail = ", ".join(f"{n}: {a}/{b} shards absent"
                       for n, (a, b) in missing.items())
    raise FileNotFoundError(
        f"manifest references shards that do not exist ({detail}). "
        "Rebuild the manifests, or pass allow_missing=True to proceed "
        "with a deliberately reduced mix.")

The escape hatch remains, because there are legitimate reasons to train on a subset, such as a smoke test on one downloaded shard. However it now has to be requested by name at the call site, where it is visible in a code review, rather than being the default that arrives when nobody is watching.3 allow_missing=True also stores the missing-shard tally on the loader, so a run that deliberately proceeds with a reduced mix still carries a record of what it dropped.

The third part is telemetry. The loader exposes both the mix it is producing and the mix it was asked for, and the training loop reports both:

"realised_mix": loader.realised_mix(),
# both, so the monitor can gate on drift rather than on a
# human noticing that a share looks wrong
"target_mix":   loader.target_mix(),

realised_mix() is computed from a per-source token counter incremented as sequences are emitted, so it is the outcome and not the intention. Publishing the target alongside it makes drift a subtraction rather than an inference. This is the same correction we made when router entropy passed a collapsing run: measure the outcome, publish it next to what was asked for, and do not require a person to remember the right value.4 The reporting cadence is ten steps for most of the run, with the first hundred steps at every step and steps 100 to 1,000 at every fifth, because that is when problems appear. At 131,072 tokens per step, a ten-step interval puts 1.3 million tokens between telemetry points.

The evidence that it works

r1's stable phase, realised against target, from the run's own telemetry:

sourcetargetrealised
fineweb-edu59.5%59.5%
web-diverse25.5%25.5%
code-python10.0%10.0%
finemath3.0%3.0%
open-web-math2.0%2.0%

The realised mix tracks the target to three decimal places, held through the stable phase to step 32,430 and reported across all 38,147 steps. That agreement is a check that the loader draws from the sources it claims to, rather than a result about the data.

The transferable part

Graceful degradation is a good default in a serving system. A recommendation service that loses one feature store and returns slightly worse recommendations is behaving correctly, because the alternative is returning nothing.

An experiment is not a serving system. When the thing degrading is the experimental setup, a fallback converts a loud failure into a silent one, and silent failures in a $252.35 run are paid for at the end. The loader that skipped missing shards and renormalised applied a serving instinct to a measurement instrument, and it very nearly cost the run its meaning.

Two rules came out of this. Missing inputs raise, and the permission to continue without them is a named argument at the call site. And anything the run is configured to do gets measured and published next to the configuration, so that agreement is observed rather than trusted. Both are cheap: an if statement, and two lines in a telemetry dictionary.

The three distributed bugs in the sharding chapter share this shape, each producing a run that completes and is quietly wrong. This one would have been the hardest to find afterwards, because a model trained entirely on maths would still have produced a falling loss, a full set of checkpoints, and twenty-four benchmark evaluations nobody would have had a reason to doubt.

Section 03 · Making It Train

14. The released code cannot train

⏱ 11 min 📊 5 figures 4 bugs

An assert against training mode, a NotImplementedError in the MoE forward, a balancer bias that is never initialised, and a timestep bias left as uninitialised memory that returned NaN on one rung and trained fine on another.

Moonshot released Kimi K3 with working modeling code. It loads the checkpoint, it runs a forward pass, it generates text, and every part of it is correct for the thing it was written to do. It cannot train, and it cannot train for four separate reasons, none of which is documented anywhere in the repository.

We found all four in one afternoon, before a training loop existed, by running a GPU smoke test: build each rung of the ladder from scratch, take two optimizer steps on a short sequence, and assert that the loss is finite, starts near ln(163,840) = 12.007 and goes down. That is a small amount of work to arrange, and it is the reason this chapter is about four bugs rather than about a run that died at hour nine.

The four have a common cause, and it is worth naming before the details. Released inference code is code that has only ever been exercised in one mode, by one caller, with every tensor loaded from a checkpoint. Anything that matters only in the other mode was never wrong in a way anyone would notice.

Blocker one: the router asserts it is not training

KimiMoEGate.forward computes its logits, applies the sigmoid, and then, immediately before top-k selection, does this:

# select top-k experts
assert not self.training

That is the whole blocker. Calling model.train() and running a forward pass raises AssertionError with no message. It is the friendliest of the four, because it fails on the first forward, in the first second, with a stack trace pointing at the exact line.

Why the assert is there is not stated, and we did not ask. The reasonable reading is that the gate's training path was never written, so the author blocked the door rather than leaving a silently-wrong path open. Our replacement, TrainableMoEGate in train/moe_train.py, copies the selection arithmetic from the reference line for line so that inference behaviour is unchanged, and adds the training path: load statistics, entropy, and the balancer state.

Blocker two: there is no differentiable dispatch

The second blocker sits one level up, in the sparse block that owns the experts:

if not self.training:
    y = self.moe_infer(hidden_states, topk_idx, topk_weight)
else:
    raise NotImplementedError("Training mode is not supported in KimiSparseMoeBlock")

moe_infer is the only dispatch in the file, and it is decorated @torch.no_grad(). Removing the NotImplementedError and calling it anyway would not help: nothing inside it carries a gradient, so the experts would produce outputs and receive none of the loss.

Dispatch is the operation that takes a batch of tokens, each routed to six of 256 experts, and arranges them so each expert can process its own tokens as one contiguous matrix. The reference does it by sorting the flattened expert indices, gathering the tokens into that order, running each expert on its slice, and then writing the results back with an in-place index assignment into a torch.empty_like buffer.

The rewrite keeps the same arithmetic and changes two things. Nothing runs under no_grad, and the scatter back is done with an inverse permutation and a gather rather than an in-place write into uninitialised memory:

n_tok, k = topk_idx.shape
flat = topk_idx.reshape(-1)
order = flat.argsort()
sorted_tokens = hidden_states[order // k]        # differentiable gather
counts = torch.bincount(flat, minlength=len(self.experts))

chunks = []
start = 0
for i, c in enumerate(counts.tolist()):
    if c == 0:
        continue
    chunks.append(self.experts[i](sorted_tokens[start:start + c]))
    start += c
outs = torch.cat(chunks, dim=0)

inv = torch.empty_like(order)
inv[order] = torch.arange(order.numel(), device=order.device)
y = outs[inv]                                     # undo the sort, by gather

An index assignment into a fresh empty tensor is a poor autograd citizen: the buffer starts as garbage, the write is in place, and the gradient has to be threaded back through an operation whose output partly does not come from any input. A gather has none of those properties and is exactly as fast.

That Python expert loop is the version we started with, and it is also the single largest throughput problem in the project. Replacing it with a batched dispatch was worth 17.5x, taking MFU from 0.1% to 4.1% — measured on r2, the rung above ours, since that is where the MFU work was done. It has its own chapter in Making It Fast. For now the loop is correct, which is what the smoke test needed it to be.

Blocker three: the balancer does not exist

K3's config names its load-balancing method in one field, topk_method: noaux_tc, and the released gate declares the tensor that method writes to:

self.e_score_correction_bias = nn.Parameter(
    torch.empty(self.num_experts),
)
self.reset_parameters()

reset_parameters initialises self.weight and stops. The bias is left as torch.empty, and nothing in the file ever adds anything to it.

For inference this is complete. from_pretrained overwrites every parameter with a loaded tensor, so the bias arrives fully formed, holding whatever values Moonshot's own training produced.1 This is the mechanism behind three of the four blockers. A parameter that is always overwritten by loaded weights can be left uninitialised with no observable consequence, and a module's _init_weights can cover only the layer types the author happened to think about, because in the checkpoint-loading path it does not run on anything that matters. For pretraining from scratch, the tensor holds uninitialised memory on step 0 and never changes afterwards, which means routing is decided partly by whatever was in that page for the entire run.

So the balancer had to be written. The rule is one line, inherited by K3 from DeepSeek-V3:

bias_i += gamma * sign(mean_load - load_i)

Our implementation declares the bias with torch.zeros, sets requires_grad_(False), and holds it out of AdamW's parameter groups. It is updated by rule, not by gradient, and letting the optimizer see it would apply weight decay and momentum to a quantity whose update is already fully specified. The two rules would then disagree about where the bias should be.

Choosing gamma is a measurement rather than a default, and it is the subject of the chapter on expert collapse, where the value inherited from DeepSeek-V3 turns out to be wrong for our batch size by a factor of ten. The point here is narrower: without this code, noaux_tc is a string in a config file.

Blocker four: a bias that is whatever was in that allocation

The fourth is the one worth spending time on, because it is the only one that does not announce itself.

KimiDeltaAttention builds two raw parameters by hand. One of them is initialised in place:

self.A_log = torch.nn.Parameter(torch.log(torch.empty(
    self.num_heads, dtype=torch.float32).uniform_(1, 16)))

...

self.dt_bias = nn.Parameter(
    torch.empty(projection_size, dtype=torch.float32))

A_log calls .uniform_(1, 16) on its empty tensor and is fine. dt_bias, eleven lines later in the same constructor, does not. And KimiPreTrainedModel._init_weights, which is the mechanism that would ordinarily catch it, handles nn.Linear and nn.Embedding and nothing else. A bare nn.Parameter is neither.

dt_bias is the timestep bias handed to the gated delta-rule kernel, one of nine KDA layers in r1. It is not a decorative term. It sits inside a softplus and sets the effective step size of the recurrence, so its magnitude controls how fast the linear-attention state decays.

Now consider what torch.empty actually returns. It allocates memory and does not write to it, so the tensor contains whatever bytes were already at that address, reinterpreted as float32. What those bytes are depends on the allocator, on what the process did earlier, on the CUDA caching allocator's block reuse, and on nothing you control. There are three ways this can go, and we saw two of them in the same smoke test:

  • The bytes decode to inf or NaN, or to values large enough that the kernel overflows. The loss is NaN on step 0 and the run is obviously broken.
  • The bytes decode to finite but wrong values. The model trains, the loss falls, every dashboard is green, and the recurrence has a step size nobody chose. There is no error and no alarm.
  • The bytes happen to be small and roughly sane. Nothing observable is wrong.

Rung r2 trained normally while r1 returned NaN from step 0, from the same code, in the same smoke run. The two models differ only in width and depth, so they allocate different-sized tensors in a different order, and their dt_bias allocations landed on different garbage. Had the ladder been r2-only, this bug would have shipped into the run.

The middle outcome is the reason this bug deserves a chapter section rather than a line in a changelog. A crash is a bug report. A silent quality regression is a model that is a little worse than it should be for a reason that will never be found, because there is nothing to search for. We would have spent the money, gotten a slightly worse loss curve, and attributed it to scale.

The fix, and the check that is not a fix

train/init_patch.py initialises dt_bias the way Mamba and GatedDeltaNet initialise their timestep parameters: sample a timestep uniformly in log-space over [1e-3, 1e-1], then invert the softplus so that softplus(dt_bias) covers that range.

dt = torch.exp(
    torch.rand(p.shape, device=p.device, dtype=torch.float32)
    * (torch.log(torch.tensor(dt_max)) - torch.log(torch.tensor(dt_min)))
    + torch.log(torch.tensor(dt_min))
).clamp(min=floor)
# inverse softplus: b = dt + log(-expm1(-dt))
p.copy_((dt + torch.log(-torch.expm1(-dt))).to(p.dtype))

Log-space rather than linear space, because the quantity being sampled spans two orders of magnitude and a linear sample would put almost all of the mass at the fast end. The inverse softplus is there because the kernel applies softplus to this parameter, so what we want to control is the distribution after that transform, not before it.

The second half of the file is not a fix. It is a check that runs before step 0:

def assert_initialised(model) -> dict:
    bad = []
    for name, t in list(model.named_parameters()) + list(model.named_buffers()):
        if t.is_floating_point() and not torch.isfinite(t).all():
            bad.append(name)
    if bad:
        raise ValueError(f"non-finite values in a freshly initialised model: {bad[:10]}")

Every parameter and buffer must be finite before the first forward pass. It costs one pass over the parameters, and it converts "the loss is NaN somewhere" into "this named tensor is bad", which is a different quality of debugging problem.

It is also incomplete, and the docstring in the file says so. Uninitialised memory is usually finite garbage rather than inf, so this check catches the loud outcome and misses the quiet one. It exists because the next uninitialised tensor will not announce itself either, and a cheap check that catches one case in three is worth having when the cost of the miss is a run that finishes and is wrong.2 The obvious stronger check is to compare every parameter's statistics against what the initialisation scheme should have produced, which would catch finite garbage as well. We did not build it. assert_initialised has caught nothing since init_dt_bias was written, which is the correct outcome for a guard and also the reason it is difficult to justify extending.

Two more traps in the same family

Neither of the next two is in the modeling code's arithmetic, and both belong to the same class: something that works in the released setting and quietly does not in ours.

transformers must be pinned to 4.57.6. The Kimi modeling code imports OutputRecorder at module scope. Version 5.x of transformers removed it, and a requirement of transformers>=4.56 resolves to 5.x, so the import fails before any model is constructed. A module-scope import error is at least a fast failure, but it is the kind of failure that appears weeks later on a fresh container image while the original environment continues to work, and it is worth a pin rather than a range.

gradient_checkpointing_enable() is a silent no-op on this modeling code. The model declares supports_gradient_checkpointing = True. KimiModel.__init__ sets self.gradient_checkpointing = False. The decoder loop then calls decoder_layer(...) directly, and there is no _gradient_checkpointing_func anywhere in the file. The flag is written and never read. KimiDecoderLayer is a plain nn.Module rather than transformers' GradientCheckpointingLayer, which is the only generic mechanism in the pinned version, so nothing else picks it up either.

Calling it produces the log line "use_cache=True is incompatible with gradient checkpointing", which is precisely the reassuring message you would expect if it were working.

The tell was in our own benchmark data and went unread for days. At 8,192 tokens per microbatch, peak memory read 76.7 GB with checkpointing off and 76.8 GB with it on. Activation checkpointing that is running trades memory for recomputation; it cannot leave memory unchanged. Identical memory is the signature of a no-op. The conclusion originally drawn from that grid, that checkpointing was slower per token but bought a larger microbatch and therefore won, compared two runs that differed only in batch size.

Real checkpointing needed one non-obvious thing beyond wrapping the layers: cfg.use_cache = False. The KV cache is mutated inside the forward pass, so the recomputed forward sees different state than the original and torch.utils.checkpoint raises CheckpointError. Setting use_cache=False was the one thing the no-op call had actually been doing.3 A related shim belongs to the same family and is described in the MFU chapters: MLA runs on an sdpa implementation registered under the flash_attention_2 key, because the model hard-forces that string and the asymmetric-head-dim padding only runs on that branch. It is numerically identical and slower, which means every MFU figure in this book understates what flash attention would give.

What the smoke test bought

All four blockers were found by smoke_all: build every rung, two steps, assert the loss is finite, starts near 12.007 and decreases. It takes a few minutes of GPU time and it was written before the training loop.

Three of the four would have been found anyway, in the first second of the first run, by a crash. The fourth would not. On r2 it does not crash, and on r1 it crashes only sometimes, so a project that smoke-tested one rung on one day had a real chance of carrying an uninitialised timestep bias into a multi-day run. What the smoke test bought was not the discovery of the loud bugs; it was running the same code on three models on the same afternoon, which is what made the non-deterministic one visible as non-determinism rather than as a mystery.4 The build path was later hardened for the same reason. train/build.py is now the only way any model is constructed, and it raises if a patch matched zero modules rather than proceeding. A patch that silently matched nothing produces a run that carries more memory than it should and gives up the fused kernel's 1.38x, with no error, which is the same failure shape as everything in this chapter.

The general form of the rule is this. Code released for inference has been exercised in one mode with every tensor loaded from disk. Before training with it, check the assumptions that only the other mode makes: which parameters are constructed rather than loaded, which branches are gated on self.training, which decorators suppress gradients, and which flags are written without being read. Every one of the four blockers, and both of the traps after them, is on that list.

The next chapter builds the training loop around this patched model: the WSD schedule, 131,072 tokens per step, and a spike protocol written down before the run rather than during it.

Section 03 · Making It Train

15. The training loop

⏱ 14 min 📊 7 figures WSD

Warmup, stable and decay instead of cosine; 131,072 tokens per step from 4,096 x 4 x 8; a learning rate transferred from one measured sweep; and the spike protocol written down before the run rather than at 3am.

A pretraining run is one loop body executed a very large number of times. Ours executed it 38,147 times over roughly 55 hours on a single H200, and every iteration did the same six things: read a batch, compute a loss, decide whether to trust that loss, take an optimizer step, nudge the router's balancing biases, and occasionally tell someone outside the process what happened.

The interesting design decisions are all in the fourth and sixth of those. This chapter is about the schedule that set the learning rate, the batch arithmetic that fed the loop, the protocol that decided when a step was not to be trusted, and the telemetry contract that made the run observable from outside. The chapter before this one covered the four blockers that had to be fixed before any of this could run at all. The chapter after it covers the router balancer whose update lives inside step five.

Warmup, stable, decay

The learning rate schedule almost every tutorial reaches for is cosine decay: start low, warm up, then follow a cosine curve down to some floor at the end of training. It works well and it has one property that is easy to overlook. Cosine commits you to a total step count on step one. The value of the cosine at step n depends on the total, so if you decide at step 20,000 that the run deserves another two billion tokens, you cannot simply continue. The schedule you have been following was shaped for a different endpoint, and extending it means the learning rate you have already applied was wrong for the run you are now doing.

We used WSD — warmup, stable, decay — instead. The learning rate ramps linearly over the first 2% of steps, holds at its peak for the long middle, then decays linearly over the final 15% to a floor of 10% of peak.

def wsd_lr(step, total, peak, warmup_frac=0.02, decay_frac=0.15, min_frac=0.1):
    """Warmup -> stable -> linear decay to `min_frac` x peak."""
    warm = max(1, int(total * warmup_frac))
    decay_start = int(total * (1.0 - decay_frac))
    if step < warm:
        return peak * (step + 1) / warm
    if step < decay_start:
        return peak
    frac = (step - decay_start) / max(1, total - decay_start)
    return peak * (1.0 - (1.0 - min_frac) * min(1.0, frac))

For our run, total = 38,147, so warmup ran for int(38,147 x 0.02) = 762 steps and the first step's learning rate was 6.00e-4 / 762 = 7.87e-7. The run's own step-0 telemetry recorded 7.87e-7, which is a small check that the schedule the loop applied is the schedule written above rather than a different one with the same name.

The reason to prefer WSD is what happens to a checkpoint taken during the stable phase. Its learning rate is the peak, and the peak does not depend on the total step count, so that checkpoint can be branched and trained further under any endpoint you later choose. A run that turns out to be worth extending can be extended. A run that turns out not to be can be decayed early from wherever it has reached, and a decayed model is a usable model rather than an interrupted one.

That property matters here because the constraint on this project was the budget, not the schedule. The whole ladder existed to price a flagship run whose token budget was still being revised, and the token budget was revised, twice. A schedule that had to be declared correctly on step one would have made each revision a restart.

The decay is not merely a cooldown. Loss falls fastest in the decay phase in most WSD runs, and it did in ours: the r1 evaluation series shows held-out perplexity dropping from 15.84 at 4.273B tokens to 13.61 at 5.000B, which is the steepest stretch of the whole run and sits entirely after the decay boundary. The benchmarks chapter takes that curve apart properly.

The anneal and the decay share a boundary

The decay phase is also where the data mix changes. Our corpus was built as two disjoint manifests, 947 stable shards and 94 decay shards, and the decay manifest is weighted toward maths, code and reasoning-heavy text. Both switches are driven by the same predicate:

def in_decay_phase(step, total, decay_frac=0.15):
    return step >= int(total * (1.0 - decay_frac))
want_decay = in_decay_phase(step, total_steps)
if want_decay and phase != "decay" and "decay" in loaders:
    phase = "decay"
    loader = loaders["decay"]
    print(f"[step {step}] entering DECAY phase — switching to the anneal mix")

r1 crossed that boundary at step 32,430, having seen 4.25 billion tokens.1 The predicate flips at int(38,147 x 0.85) = 32,424. The run's history records the transition at 32,430 because telemetry is reported every ten steps after step 1,000, so the first report that saw phase == "decay" was the one at 32,430. The switch happened six steps earlier than the number in the log, which is worth knowing before anyone tries to reconcile the two. From that step onward it trained on the decay mix at a falling learning rate, and the two changes are deliberately not separable in this run. We did not run an ablation with the anneal and the decay at different boundaries, so we cannot attribute the shape of the final loss curve to one rather than the other, and the evaluation chapter says so where it reports the numbers.

The batch, and the number that is not the batch

Four numbers multiply together to make one optimizer step:

sequence length4,096
sequences per microbatch4
microbatch16,384 tokens
gradient accumulation8
tokens per optimizer step131,072

Multiplying that by the 38,147 steps gives 5,000,003,584 tokens, which is where the run's headline token count comes from. It is not a round number because the step count was chosen to reach five billion, not the other way around: round(5e9 / 131,072) = 38,147, and 38,147 steps of 131,072 tokens overshoots five billion by 3,584 tokens.

Now the distinction that the entire next section of this book rests on. Gradient accumulation raises the tokens per optimizer step. It does not raise the microbatch, and the microbatch is what sets MFU.

The GPU never sees 131,072 tokens at once. It sees 16,384 tokens, computes a forward and backward pass on them, keeps the gradients, and does it again seven more times before the optimizer runs. Every kernel launch, every padded expert buffer, every activation tensor is sized by 16,384. Accumulation amortises only the work that happens once per optimizer step — the AdamW update, the gradient clip, the balancer update, the telemetry — and that work is a small fraction of the total.

This is not an argument, it is a measurement, and it was taken on r2, the rung above ours. When the fused situ kernel freed 40 GB, the resulting MFU gain came entirely from being able to raise the microbatch from 16,384 to 24,576 tokens: at equal batch the kernel scored 5.5% against 5.6%, and at the larger microbatch it scored 7.7% at 41,294 tokens per second. The kernel won by allowing a bigger microbatch, not by being faster. Accumulation could not have produced that gain at any setting.2 Which is why the MFU chapters are all about memory. Microbatch is bounded by memory, memory is dominated by activations, and the levers that matter are the ones that make activations smaller. Accumulation is orthogonal to all of it, and it is the first thing people suggest.

For r1 the arithmetic runs as follows. The run sustained 28,936 tokens per second near the end, and MFU is 6 x active_params x tokens_per_second / peak_FLOPs, so 6 x 145e6 x 28,936 / 989e12 = 2.5%. That is the number the loop reported, and it is measured on the real run rather than in a benchmark harness. The same model measured in the harness at a 16,384-token microbatch reached 66,877 tokens per second and 5.9% MFU. Both figures are real; the gap between them is the subject of its own discussion in the opening chapter.

One learning rate, measured once

The peak learning rate came from a single sweep, run at r1's width on short runs: four rates, 600 steps each, on identical data with an identical seed.

lrs = [1e-4, 3e-4, 6e-4, 1e-3]
handles = [run.spawn(which, steps, seq_len, batch, 1, lr, "H200", 0, "",
                     True, None) for lr in lrs]

Short runs are enough for this question because learning-rate problems announce themselves early: too low and the loss plateaus high within a few hundred steps, too high and it spikes or goes to NaN. The sweep recorded zero loss spikes across all four rates, and the best final loss was at 6.00e-4.

Every other rung's learning rate is that one measurement transferred by width:

# r1 hidden  512 -> 6.00e-4      r3 hidden 1024 -> 4.24e-4
# r2 hidden  768 -> 4.90e-4      flagship 1536 -> 3.46e-4
RUNG_LR = {"r1": 6.00e-4, "r2": 4.90e-4, "r3": 4.24e-4}

The rule is 6e-4 * sqrt(512 / width). This is standard-parameterisation transfer, not muP, and it is the weakest assumption in the recipe. Under standard parameterisation the optimal learning rate is expected to fall roughly as the inverse square root of width, which is a rule of thumb with empirical support and not a theorem. muP is the principled alternative: it reparameterises initialisation and per-layer learning rates so that the optimal rate is width-invariant by construction, which would let one sweep transfer exactly instead of approximately. We did not implement it.

Stating this plainly matters more than it might appear, because the flagship's 3.46e-4 was going to be applied to a 35.56-billion-parameter run on two B300s at $7.10 an hour each, derived from one 600-step sweep on a model with a third of the width and a fraction of a percent of the parameters. The flagship failed for an unrelated reason — 69.5% dead experts and a stall at step 890 of 76,294 — so this assumption was never tested at the scale where it would have mattered most.

Weight decay, and a deviation recorded rather than hidden

AdamW ran with betas=(0.9, 0.95), eps=1e-8 and weight decay 0.1, applied through two parameter groups:

def param_groups(model, skip_ids, weight_decay):
    """Two groups: decay the matrices, do not decay anything 1-D."""
    decay, no_decay = [], []
    for p in model.parameters():
        if not p.requires_grad or id(p) in skip_ids:
            continue
        (decay if p.dim() >= 2 else no_decay).append(p)
    return [{"params": decay, "weight_decay": weight_decay},
            {"params": no_decay, "weight_decay": 0.0}]

Everything one-dimensional — RMSNorm gains, biases, KDA's timestep bias — receives no weight decay. Decaying a normalisation gain pulls toward zero the very scale the layer exists to set. Every large open training run does this, so it is a default rather than a tuning choice.

The part worth recording is that the r1 learning-rate sweep that produced 6.00e-4 ran with a single parameter group and decay on everything. The interaction between weight decay on 1-D parameters and the optimal learning rate is second-order, and we did not re-run the sweep after splitting the groups. It is a difference between the conditions the constant was measured under and the conditions it was used under, which is exactly the class of thing that is easy to leave in a code comment nobody reads.

The skip_ids set holds the router's e_score_correction_bias tensors, which are excluded from the optimizer entirely. Their update rule is already fully specified by the balancer, and handing them to AdamW would have momentum and weight decay fighting a rule that is not a gradient. The expert-collapse chapter covers what that rule does.

The spike protocol, written before the run

Loss spikes are a normal event in large pretraining runs and a fatal one if handled badly. The policy was written into the loop before the first real run rather than improvised during it:

  • A step whose loss is NaN or infinite is skipped.
  • A step whose loss exceeds 1.5x a running EMA of recent losses is skipped.
  • A skip means gradients are dropped and the optimizer does not step. One batch of data is lost.
  • Five consecutive skips abort the run and ask for a rollback rather than grinding on through a divergence.

Two details in the implementation are less obvious than the policy.

if self.ema is not None and step > self.warmup_steps and loss > self.ema * self.ratio:
    ...
    return True, f"loss {loss:.3f} > {self.ratio}x EMA {self.ema:.3f}"
# only healthy steps update the baseline, or one spike drags the EMA up
# and the next spike looks normal
self.ema = loss if self.ema is None else 0.98 * self.ema + 0.02 * loss

First, the EMA is updated only on steps that were not skipped. If a spike were folded into the baseline, the baseline would rise toward the spike, and the second spike of a developing divergence would sit under the threshold that the first one broke. The detector would grow more permissive exactly as the run grew less healthy.

Second, the guard's EMA is training state and is saved in the checkpoint. A resume that restarts it at None disables spike detection for the whole of the guard's warmup window and then re-derives a baseline from whatever the loss happens to be at that moment. The run would come back from a crash with its safety system off, at the point in its life when a crash has just demonstrated that something is wrong.3 The checkpoint carries model weights, optimizer moments, the dataloader cursor, all four RNG states, tokens seen, spend to date and the spike-guard EMA. The checkpointing chapter argues that a checkpoint holding anything less is not a checkpoint of the run, and shows the resume-equivalence test that proved this one is.

r1 skipped zero steps across all 38,147. No NaN, no loss above 1.5x its EMA, no aborts. That is a statement about how uneventful the run was rather than about how good the protocol is, and the protocol is untested by it. The place we know the machinery is needed is distributed training, where every rank must reach the same skip decision or the optimizer desynchronises. r1 never met that case: it fit on one H200 and was never sharded, so its guard only ever ran on a single rank. The all-reduce of the loss before the guard sees it was written for the models above r1, and it was on the flagship's two B300s that the desynchronisation it prevents was actually diagnosed. The sharding chapter goes through that bug and the two others alongside it.

The telemetry contract

Every ten steps, rank 0 assembles a dictionary and hands it to the monitor.4 Logging is dense early and sparse later: every step below step 100, every fifth step below step 1,000, then every log_every steps. At 131,072 tokens per step, a flat interval of ten steps puts 1.31 million tokens between reports, which at the run's sustained 28,936 tokens per second is about forty-five seconds of blank dashboard — tolerable in the middle of the run, and far too coarse over the first hundred steps, which is when problems appear. The call is .spawn() rather than .remote(), so a slow monitor cannot throttle training, and the whole thing is wrapped in a bare except that prints and continues. Telemetry observes the run; it must never be able to gate it. The fields are fixed, and choosing them was a design decision rather than an afterthought:

m = {
    "stage": stage, "step": step, "phase": phase,
    "total_steps": total_steps,
    "tokens_seen": tokens_seen,
    "target_tokens": total_steps * step_tokens,
    "loss": loss, "lr": lr, "grad_norm": float(gnorm),
    "mfu": mfu_of(active_params, tok_s, peak_flops),
    "tok_per_s": tok_s,
    "router_entropy_min": router.get("router_entropy_min"),
    "dead_expert_frac": router.get("dead_expert_frac"),
    "load_imbalance": router.get("load_imbalance_max"),
    "spikes_skipped": guard.total_skipped,
    "spend_usd": spend_usd_start + elapsed_h * usd_per_hour,
    "status": "running",
    "eta": f"{(total_steps - step) * dt / 3600:.1f}h",
    ...
}

Three of those fields are worth pointing at.

dead_expert_frac is there because router entropy was not enough. That is the whole subject of the next chapter: at step 20 of the first real run, entropy read 0.99 out of 1.0 while 94.5% of experts were receiving no tokens. Both metrics are reported here, and the gate is on the second one.

tokens_seen against target_tokens is reported rather than derived on the dashboard, because the two can disagree. target_tokens is total_steps x step_tokens, and tokens_seen accumulates what the loader actually delivered, multiplied by the world size on a sharded run. A mismatch between them is a real bug rather than a display quirk.

spend_usd is tracked by the training loop itself, from its own elapsed time and the hourly rate, and it is carried across a resume through the checkpoint. This is what makes a budget gate possible at all: a process that knows what it has spent can be told to stop at a number. An earlier version reset it to zero on resume, which meant an over-budget gate could never fire on a run that had been restarted even once, which is precisely the long run such a gate exists to protect.

Here are the first and last reports of the run, side by side.

fieldstep 0step 38,140
loss12.1002.623
lr7.87e-76.07e-5
tok/s71628,936
mfu—2.5%
dead_expert_frac0.0%0.0%
load_imbalance2.364.0
router_entropy_min—0.973
grad_norm—0.299
spend_usd~0$252.35

The step-0 loss is the one worth checking rather than celebrating. A model that has learned nothing spreads its probability mass evenly over the vocabulary, and the cross-entropy of a uniform distribution over 163,840 tokens is ln(163,840) = 12.007. We measured 12.100. If that number had come out at 6, the loss would have been computed over the wrong axis; if it had come out at 30, the initialisation would have been wrong. It is the cheapest sanity check available in this entire project and it costs one step.

The step-0 throughput of 716 tokens per second against a sustained 28,936 is the allocator growing and the kernels autotuning. This is why MFU measurement discipline in this project always discards the first two steps.

What the loop is for

Almost nothing in this chapter is novel. WSD is standard practice, gradient accumulation is a two-line pattern, spike skipping is folklore that several open runs have published, and no weight decay on 1-D parameters is in every large open recipe.

What made the difference was writing them down before the run rather than during it, and reporting enough state that a failure would be visible from outside the process. The spike protocol never fired. The budget gate never fired. The dead-expert gate never fired. On a run that finishes cleanly, all of that machinery looks like overhead, and the only way to know whether it was worth building is to compare against the flagship, which had the same machinery, collapsed with 69.5% dead experts and load imbalance between 74 and 85, and was stopped at step 890 of 76,294 having spent $48.33. It stopped at 1.2% of its step count, and it stopped because the gates fired.

The loop's job is not to make training work. It is to make the difference between a run that is working and a run that is not visible before the money is gone.

Section 03 · Making It Train

16. Expert collapse, and the metric that missed it

⏱ 9 min 📊 5 figures 94.5%

Twenty steps in, 94.5% of experts were receiving no tokens while router entropy read 0.99 out of 1.0. Why entropy passes a collapsing run, and the four-value sweep that fixed it.

A mixture-of-experts layer holds many small feed-forward networks and sends each token to a few of them. Ours holds 256 and sends each token to 6. That arrangement is what lets a 1.02-billion-parameter model cost only 145 million parameters of compute per token, and it comes with a failure mode that has no equivalent in a dense model: the router can decide it likes a handful of experts and stop using the rest.

Twenty steps into the first real training run, 94.5% of our experts were receiving zero tokens.

What makes this worth a chapter is not that it happened. Every mixture-of-experts model does this early in training, and the literature says so. What makes it worth a chapter is that the metric we had chosen in advance to detect exactly this condition was reading 0.99 out of 1.0 while it happened, and would have let the run continue to completion.

Why entropy misses it

The router produces a score for every expert for every token, and our chosen health metric was the entropy of those scores. Entropy is high when a distribution is spread out and low when it concentrates, so low entropy should mean the router has picked favourites. The reasoning is sound, and the implementation was correct.

The problem is which distribution gets measured. Entropy was computed over the mean routing scores across the batch, and those means stay close to uniform even when routing has collapsed completely. Selection is top-k, and top-k does not care about the size of a gap. If expert 7 scores 0.51 and expert 118 scores 0.49 for every token in the batch, the mean scores are nearly identical, the entropy over them is nearly maximal, and expert 118 never gets selected once.
